  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess what finally made me agree to come in is that I’ve sort of stopped caring about the things that used to keep me steady. I’m 51, I’ve always been the kind of guy who keeps busy—work, projects, commitments, hobbies—and usually that’s helped me feel like myself.

But lately, even the things I used to genuinely look forward to just feel like chores. Sailing races, for example… I used to plan my week around them, check the weather, mess with gear, all of that. Now I find myself putting it off or making excuses. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society—I used to enjoy being useful there, talking with people, keeping up with the little routines. And even being out in the woodshop, tinkering with something, which used to calm me down… now it’s like I look at the tools and just think, “Not today.”

It’s not that I suddenly hate any of it. It’s more like the interest got turned way down, and I can’t seem to get it back. That’s what worried me enough to come in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad, exactly… I don’t know if I’d call it that most of the time. It’s not like I’m walking around crying or feeling openly miserable all day.

It’s more this constant low-level dread, if that makes sense. Like there’s this weight in the background, and it keeps turning into guilt. I keep thinking I should have seen one more thing coming, one more risk, one more problem before it happened. And people tell me, “The controls were in place, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really land. I can hear that logically, but emotionally it just sort of bounces off.

So empty, maybe… but mostly it feels like dread and guilt following me around, even when there’s no immediate reason for it right in front of me.

Sailing races, for example… I used to plan my week around them, check the weather, mess with gear, all of that. Now I find myself putting it off or making excuses. And even being out in the woodshop, tinkering with something, which used to calm me down… now it’s like I look at the tools and just think, “Not today.”\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate any of it. It’s more like the interest got turned way down, and I can’t seem to get it back. That’s what worried me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly… I don’t know if I’d call it that most of the time. Yes… definitely. That’s probably the clearest thing I can point to, honestly.

It’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more that the enjoyment just sort of drained out of them. Sailing races used to be a big thing for me—I’d look forward to them, think about the weather, the equipment, who’d be there, all the little details. Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.

Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”

And the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.

Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner or I can finish a difficult project milestone, and I know those things should feel good, but mostly I just feel flat and a little removed from it.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n  description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: A few times I've taken the boat out alone when the weather was turning, not exactly hoping for trouble, but ignoring the kind of judgment I used to pride myself on having.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If someone drops a metal ladder or a truck gate slams, I jump hard before I can stop myself, and it takes me longer than it should to get my heart rate back down.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still doing my job, but I’ve started delegating site visits I should be leading and avoiding client discussions that touch the accident, which makes me worry I’m not giving the project the steady direction it needs.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, a little over a year now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / project_management_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me agree to come in is that I’ve sort of stopped caring about the things that used to keep me steady. I’m 51, I’ve always been the kind of guy who keeps busy—work, projects, commitments, hobbies—and usually that’s helped me feel like myself.\n\nBut lately, even the things I used to genuinely look forward to just feel like chores. Sailing races, for example… I used to plan my week around them, check the weather, mess with gear, all of that. Now I find myself putting it off or making excuses. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society—I used to enjoy being useful there, talking with people, keeping up with the little routines. And even being out in the woodshop, tinkering with something, which used to calm me down… now it’s like I look at the tools and just think, “Not today.”\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate any of it. It’s more like the interest got turned way down, and I can’t seem to get it back. That’s what worried me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly… I don’t know if I’d call it that most of the time. It’s not like I’m walking around crying or feeling openly miserable all day.\n\nIt’s more this constant low-level dread, if that makes sense. Like there’s this weight in the background, and it keeps turning into guilt. I keep thinking I should have seen one more thing coming, one more risk, one more problem before it happened. And people tell me, “The controls were in place, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really land. I can hear that logically, but emotionally it just sort of bounces off.\n\nSo empty, maybe… but mostly it feels like dread and guilt following me around, even when there’s no immediate reason for it right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in pleasure from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s probably the clearest thing I can point to, honestly.\n\nIt’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more that the enjoyment just sort of drained out of them. Sailing races used to be a big thing for me—I’d look forward to them, think about the weather, the equipment, who’d be there, all the little details. Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”\n\nAnd the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, my sleep’s been different. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.

Most nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. I’ll be half-awake at first, and then all the details start coming back, and once that starts I’m just… up. I’ll lie there trying not to think about it, but that never really works.

And then I get very aware of sounds in the house or outside. The furnace clicking on, a car going by, a board creaking — little normal noises, I know that, but at that hour they feel louder than they should. So instead of getting real rest, I’m lying there listening, waiting, trying to settle myself back down. Some nights I eventually doze off again, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep.

discussions that touch the accident, which makes me worry I’m not giving the project the steady direction it needs.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, a little over a year now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / project_management_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly… I don’t know if I’d call it that most of the time. It’s not like I’m walking around crying or feeling openly miserable all day.\n\nIt’s more this constant low-level dread, if that makes sense. Like there’s this weight in the background, and it keeps turning into guilt. I keep thinking I should have seen one more thing coming, one more risk, one more problem before it happened. And people tell me, “The controls were in place, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really land. I can hear that logically, but emotionally it just sort of bounces off.\n\nSo empty, maybe… but mostly it feels like dread and guilt following me around, even when there’s no immediate reason for it right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in pleasure from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s probably the clearest thing I can point to, honestly.\n\nIt’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more that the enjoyment just sort of drained out of them. Sailing races used to be a big thing for me—I’d look forward to them, think about the weather, the equipment, who’d be there, all the little details. Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”\n\nAnd the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep’s been different. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.\n\nMost nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. I’ll be half-awake at first, and then all the details start coming back, and once that starts I’m just… up. I’ll lie there trying not to think about it, but that never really works.\n\nAnd then I get very aware of sounds in the house or outside. The furnace clicking on, a car going by, a board creaking — little normal noises, I know that, but at that hour they feel louder than they should. So instead of getting real rest, I’m lying there listening, waiting, trying to settle myself back down. Some nights I eventually doze off again, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

It’s been… a little over a year now, I think. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.

I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.

It’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.

Sailing races, for example… I used to plan my week around them, check the weather, mess with gear, all of that. Now I find myself putting it off or making excuses. Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.

Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society—I used to enjoy being useful there, talking with people, keeping up with the little routines. And even being out in the woodshop, tinkering with something, which used to calm me down… now it’s like I look at the tools and just think, “Not today.”\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate any of it. It’s more like the interest got turned way down, and I can’t seem to get it back. That’s what worried me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly… I don’t know if I’d call it that most of the time. It’s not like I’m walking around crying or feeling openly miserable all day.\n\nIt’s more this constant low-level dread, if that makes sense. Like there’s this weight in the background, and it keeps turning into guilt. I keep thinking I should have seen one more thing coming, one more risk, one more problem before it happened. And people tell me, “The controls were in place, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really land. I can hear that logically, but emotionally it just sort of bounces off.\n\nSo empty, maybe… but mostly it feels like dread and guilt following me around, even when there’s no immediate reason for it right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in pleasure from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s probably the clearest thing I can point to, honestly.\n\nIt’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more that the enjoyment just sort of drained out of them. Sailing races used to be a big thing for me—I’d look forward to them, think about the weather, the equipment, who’d be there, all the little details. Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”\n\nAnd the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep’s been different. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.\n\nMost nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. I’ll be half-awake at first, and then all the details start coming back, and once that starts I’m just… up. I’ll lie there trying not to think about it, but that never really works.\n\nAnd then I get very aware of sounds in the house or outside. The furnace clicking on, a car going by, a board creaking — little normal noises, I know that, but at that hour they feel louder than they should. So instead of getting real rest, I’m lying there listening, waiting, trying to settle myself back down. Some nights I eventually doze off again, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I think. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress from everything changing at work, and that once things settled down I’d settle down too. But it didn’t really fade the way I expected. It’s been there in one form or another since then, not necessarily at the same intensity every single day, but enough that I can’t honestly say it ever went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. But I don’t sit there thinking I’d be better off dead, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… unsettling thoughts, not something I’m seeking out or wanting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I finally agreed to come in.

I still know, intellectually, what I used to enjoy. Like sailing races — those used to be one of the things that kept me feeling like myself. I’d look forward to them, check the weather, think through the route, all of that. And my Saturday shift with the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a nice anchor in the week. Even being out in the woodshop, just tinkering with something, sanding or adjusting a piece until it was right… that was usually calming for me.

But lately it’s more like I look at those things and they feel like obligations. Chores. I keep putting them off, or I tell myself I’ll get back to them next weekend, and then I don’t. It’s not that I hate them now, exactly. I just don’t seem to care in the same way. The anticipation isn’t there. I don’t get that little lift from thinking about them. And that bothers me, because those were the things that used to keep me balanced.

Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. But I don’t sit there thinking I’d be better off dead, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… unsettling thoughts, not something I’m seeking out or wanting.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like everything is fine all the time, because I do get upset or overwhelmed sometimes, but hurting myself specifically… no. I haven’t been thinking about doing that, and I don’t have any plan or intention like that. If anything, when I get shaken up, I’m more trying to get through the moment than thinking about harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been moving unusually slowly, like people are commenting on it or I feel like I’m physically dragging in some obvious way. And I don’t think I’ve been sped up either, like talking a mile a minute or unable to sit still because my body is racing.\n\nMy thoughts can feel busy at times, but I wouldn’t describe it as “sped up” exactly. It’s more just… normal stress or trying to sort through things. But in terms of what you’re asking — moving slower or everything speeding up — no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing I’ve noticed lately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nI still know, intellectually, what I used to enjoy. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most of the time, I think. Not every single second, but it’s there in the background a lot.

It’s like this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around during the day. I keep feeling like I should have seen one more risk coming, or caught one more thing before it became a problem, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place and that I did what I was supposed to do. I can hear that logically, but it doesn’t really settle it emotionally.

So it’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly falling apart all day. I can still get through things. But it’s there enough that it colors a lot of my normal routines. I’ll be doing something ordinary and then that feeling comes back — like I missed something, like I should have anticipated more — and it’s hard to just move on from it.

It’s more like the interest got turned way down, and I can’t seem to get it back. That’s what worried me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad, exactly… I don’t know if I’d call it that most of the time. It’s not like I’m walking around crying or feeling openly miserable all day.\n\nIt’s more this constant low-level dread, if that makes sense. Like there’s this weight in the background, and it keeps turning into guilt. I keep thinking I should have seen one more thing coming, one more risk, one more problem before it happened. And people tell me, “The controls were in place, you did what you were supposed to do,” but it doesn’t really land. I can hear that logically, but emotionally it just sort of bounces off.\n\nSo empty, maybe… but mostly it feels like dread and guilt following me around, even when there’s no immediate reason for it right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in pleasure from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s probably the clearest thing I can point to, honestly.\n\nIt’s not like I made some decision to stop doing things. It’s more that the enjoyment just sort of drained out of them. Sailing races used to be a big thing for me—I’d look forward to them, think about the weather, the equipment, who’d be there, all the little details. Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”\n\nAnd the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep’s been different. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.\n\nMost nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. I’ll be half-awake at first, and then all the details start coming back, and once that starts I’m just… up. I’ll lie there trying not to think about it, but that never really works.\n\nAnd then I get very aware of sounds in the house or outside. The furnace clicking on, a car going by, a board creaking — little normal noises, I know that, but at that hour they feel louder than they should. So instead of getting real rest, I’m lying there listening, waiting, trying to settle myself back down. Some nights I eventually doze off again, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I think. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress from everything changing at work, and that once things settled down I’d settle down too. But it didn’t really fade the way I expected. It’s been there in one form or another since then, not necessarily at the same intensity every single day, but enough that I can’t honestly say it ever went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. But I don’t sit there thinking I’d be better off dead, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… unsettling thoughts, not something I’m seeking out or wanting.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like everything is fine all the time, because I do get upset or overwhelmed sometimes, but hurting myself specifically… no. I haven’t been thinking about doing that, and I don’t have any plan or intention like that. If anything, when I get shaken up, I’m more trying to get through the moment than thinking about harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been moving unusually slowly, like people are commenting on it or I feel like I’m physically dragging in some obvious way. And I don’t think I’ve been sped up either, like talking a mile a minute or unable to sit still because my body is racing.\n\nMy thoughts can feel busy at times, but I wouldn’t describe it as “sped up” exactly. It’s more just… normal stress or trying to sort through things. But in terms of what you’re asking — moving slower or everything speeding up — no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing I’ve noticed lately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nI still know, intellectually, what I used to enjoy. Like sailing races — those used to be one of the things that kept me feeling like myself. I’d look forward to them, check the weather, think through the route, all of that. And my Saturday shift with the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a nice anchor in the week. Even being out in the woodshop, just tinkering with something, sanding or adjusting a piece until it was right… that was usually calming for me.\n\nBut lately it’s more like I look at those things and they feel like obligations. Chores. I keep putting them off, or I tell myself I’ll get back to them next weekend, and then I don’t. It’s not that I hate them now, exactly. I just don’t seem to care in the same way. The anticipation isn’t there. I don’t get that little lift from thinking about them. And that bothers me, because those were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of dread and guilt are interfering with your daily life most of the time, or is it more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think. Not every single second, but it’s there in the background a lot.\n\nIt’s like this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around during the day. I keep feeling like I should have seen one more risk coming, or caught one more thing before it became a problem, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place and that I did what I was supposed to do. I can hear that logically, but it doesn’t really settle it emotionally.\n\nSo it’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly falling apart all day. I can still get through things. But it’s there enough that it colors a lot of my normal routines. I’ll be doing something ordinary and then that feeling comes back — like I missed something, like I should have anticipated more — and it’s hard to just move on from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F32.1", "F33.0", "F33.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Chores. I keep putting them off, or I tell myself I’ll get back to them next weekend, and then I don’t. It’s not that I hate them now, exactly. I just don’t seem to care in the same way. The anticipation isn’t there. I don’t get that little lift from thinking about them. And that bothers me, because those were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of dread and guilt are interfering with your daily life most of the time, or is it more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think. Not every single second, but it’s there in the background a lot.\n\nIt’s like this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around during the day. I keep feeling like I should have seen one more risk coming, or caught one more thing before it became a problem, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place and that I did what I was supposed to do. I can hear that logically, but it doesn’t really settle it emotionally.\n\nSo it’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly falling apart all day. I can still get through things. But it’s there enough that it colors a lot of my normal routines. I’ll be doing something ordinary and then that feeling comes back — like I missed something, like I should have anticipated more — and it’s hard to just move on from it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say the dread and guilt are overwhelming at times or are they more of a persistent, manageable feeling?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / project_management_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\nmanifestation: \"There's this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around, like I should have seen one more risk coming even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more persistent than overwhelming, I’d say. I mean, there are moments where it feels heavier, where it kind of rises up and I get stuck on it, but most of the time it’s not like I’m completely incapacitated by it.

It’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt sitting in the background. Like I’m going through the day and some part of me is still reviewing everything, thinking, “You should have seen one more risk. You should have caught one more thing.” And the frustrating part is that people keep telling me the controls were in place, that it wasn’t because I ignored something or failed to do what I was supposed to do. I can repeat that back to myself, but it doesn’t really make the feeling go away.

So… manageable, technically, yes. I can function. But persistent. It follows me around more than I’d like to admit.

Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”\n\nAnd the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep’s been different. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.\n\nMost nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. I’ll be half-awake at first, and then all the details start coming back, and once that starts I’m just… up. So instead of getting real rest, I’m lying there listening, waiting, trying to settle myself back down. Some nights I eventually doze off again, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I think. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress from everything changing at work, and that once things settled down I’d settle down too. But it didn’t really fade the way I expected. It’s been there in one form or another since then, not necessarily at the same intensity every single day, but enough that I can’t honestly say it ever went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. I haven’t been thinking about doing that, and I don’t have any plan or intention like that. Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\n  description: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).\n  manifestation: There's this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around, like I should have seen one more risk coming even though people keep telling me the controls were in place.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: My wife and kids can be laughing at dinner or I can description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: A few times I've taken the boat out alone when the weather was turning, not exactly hoping for trouble, but ignoring the kind of judgment I used to pride myself on having.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If someone drops a metal ladder or a truck gate slams, I jump hard before I can stop myself, and it takes me longer than it should to get my heart rate back down.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still doing my job, but I’ve started delegating site visits I should be leading and avoiding client discussions that touch the accident, which makes me worry I’m not giving the project the steady direction it needs.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, a little over a year now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / project_management_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Like sailing races — those used to be one of the things that kept me feeling like myself. I’d look forward to them, check the weather, think through the route, all of that. And my Saturday shift with the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a nice anchor in the week. Even being out in the woodshop, just tinkering with something, sanding or adjusting a piece until it was right… that was usually calming for me.\n\nBut lately it’s more like I look at those things and they feel like obligations. Chores. I keep putting them off, or I tell myself I’ll get back to them next weekend, and then I don’t. It’s not that I hate them now, exactly. I just don’t seem to care in the same way. I keep feeling like I should have seen one more risk coming, or caught one more thing before it became a problem, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place and that I did what I was supposed to do. I can hear that logically, but it doesn’t really settle it emotionally.\n\nSo it’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly falling apart all day. I can still get through things. But it’s there enough that it colors a lot of my normal routines. I’ll be doing something ordinary and then that feeling comes back — like I missed something, like I should have anticipated more — and it’s hard to just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the dread and guilt are overwhelming at times or are they more of a persistent, manageable feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s more persistent than overwhelming, I’d say. I mean, there are moments where it feels heavier, where it kind of rises up and I get stuck on it, but most of the time it’s not like I’m completely incapacitated by it.\n\nIt’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt sitting in the background. Like I’m going through the day and some part of me is still reviewing everything, thinking, “You should have seen one more risk. You should have caught one more thing.” And the frustrating part is that people keep telling me the controls were in place, that it wasn’t because I ignored something or failed to do what I was supposed to do. I can repeat that back to myself, but it doesn’t really make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… manageable, technically, yes. I can function. But persistent. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.

I mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, sure — stressful projects, family stuff, times where I was tired or discouraged or not enjoying things as much for a while. I don’t think I’d call those the same as this, though. Or at least I don’t remember them having this same kind of weight to them.

Part of me wants to say no, not like this. But then I wonder if I’m minimizing earlier periods because I got through them and moved on. I’ve always been the sort of person who just keeps functioning and tells myself it’s just a rough patch. So I don’t know if I’ve ever really stopped and labeled it as “depressed.”

But as far as I can tell, this feels different enough that it’s what got my attention. It’s not just being in a slump for a week or two. It feels more persistent, more tied into everything. So… I guess my best answer is, not that I can clearly remember in this same way.

Like there’s this weight in the background, and it keeps turning into guilt. I keep thinking I should have seen one more thing coming, one more risk, one more problem before it happened. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.\n\nMost nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress from everything changing at work, and that once things settled down I’d settle down too. But it didn’t really fade the way I expected. It’s been there in one form or another since then, not necessarily at the same intensity every single day, but enough that I can’t honestly say it ever went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. But I don’t sit there thinking I’d be better off dead, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… unsettling thoughts, not something I’m seeking out or wanting.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like everything is fine all the time, because I do get upset or overwhelmed sometimes, but hurting myself specifically… no. I haven’t been thinking about doing that, and I don’t have any plan or intention like that. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress from everything changing at work, and that once things settled down I’d settle down too. But it didn’t really fade the way I expected. It’s been there in one form or another since then, not necessarily at the same intensity every single day, but enough that I can’t honestly say it ever went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. But I don’t sit there thinking I’d be better off dead, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… unsettling thoughts, not something I’m seeking out or wanting.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like everything is fine all the time, because I do get upset or overwhelmed sometimes, but hurting myself specifically… no. I haven’t been thinking about doing that, and I don’t have any plan or intention like that. If anything, when I get shaken up, I’m more trying to get through the moment than thinking about harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been moving unusually slowly, like people are commenting on it or I feel like I’m physically dragging in some obvious way. And I don’t think I’ve been sped up either, like talking a mile a minute or unable to sit still because my body is racing.\n\nMy thoughts can feel busy at times, but I wouldn’t describe it as “sped up” exactly. It’s more just… normal stress or trying to sort through things. But in terms of what you’re asking — moving slower or everything speeding up — no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing I’ve noticed lately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nI still know, intellectually, what I used to enjoy. Like sailing races — those used to be one of the things that kept me feeling like myself. I’d look forward to them, check the weather, think through the route, all of that. And my Saturday shift with the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a nice anchor in the week. Even being out in the woodshop, just tinkering with something, sanding or adjusting a piece until it was right… that was usually calming for me.\n\nBut lately it’s more like I look at those things and they feel like obligations. Chores. I keep putting them off, or I tell myself I’ll get back to them next weekend, and then I don’t. It’s not that I hate them now, exactly. I just don’t seem to care in the same way. The anticipation isn’t there. I don’t get that little lift from thinking about them. And that bothers me, because those were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of dread and guilt are interfering with your daily life most of the time, or is it more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think. Not every single second, but it’s there in the background a lot.\n\nIt’s like this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around during the day. I keep feeling like I should have seen one more risk coming, or caught one more thing before it became a problem, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place and that I did what I was supposed to do. I can hear that logically, but it doesn’t really settle it emotionally.\n\nSo it’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly falling apart all day. I can still get through things. But it’s there enough that it colors a lot of my normal routines. I’ll be doing something ordinary and then that feeling comes back — like I missed something, like I should have anticipated more — and it’s hard to just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the dread and guilt are overwhelming at times or are they more of a persistent, manageable feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s more persistent than overwhelming, I’d say. I mean, there are moments where it feels heavier, where it kind of rises up and I get stuck on it, but most of the time it’s not like I’m completely incapacitated by it.\n\nIt’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt sitting in the background. Like I’m going through the day and some part of me is still reviewing everything, thinking, “You should have seen one more risk. You should have caught one more thing.” And the frustrating part is that people keep telling me the controls were in place, that it wasn’t because I ignored something or failed to do what I was supposed to do. I can repeat that back to myself, but it doesn’t really make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… manageable, technically, yes. I can function. But persistent. It follows me around more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, sure — stressful projects, family stuff, times where I was tired or discouraged or not enjoying things as much for a while. I don’t think I’d call those the same as this, though. Or at least I don’t remember them having this same kind of weight to them.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, not like this. But then I wonder if I’m minimizing earlier periods because I got through them and moved on. I’ve always been the sort of person who just keeps functioning and tells myself it’s just a rough patch. So I don’t know if I’ve ever really stopped and labeled it as “depressed.”\n\nBut as far as I can tell, this feels different enough that it’s what got my attention. It’s not just being in a slump for a week or two. It feels more persistent, more tied into everything. So… I guess my best answer is, not that I can clearly remember in this same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "determine the presence of anhedonia", "question": "Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, even if it's just a little bit?"}

Not really, no. I mean… maybe tiny moments here and there, but nothing I actually look forward to the way I used to.

That’s honestly part of what finally made me agree to come in. I used to have things that kind of kept me balanced. Sailing races, for one. I’d plan around them, check conditions, fuss with gear, talk strategy — even if I was busy, I wanted to be there. Even tinkering in the woodshop — that was always my way of decompressing.

Now all of that just feels like… chores. Things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I should go, or I should get out to the shop for an hour, and then I just don’t. Or if I do, I’m kind of going through the motions and waiting until I can leave. It’s not that I hate those things now. I just don’t seem to care about them the way I did, and that worries me.

Sailing races used to be a big thing for me—I’d look forward to them, think about the weather, the equipment, who’d be there, all the little details. Now I catch myself treating it like one more obligation, like something I need an excuse to get out of.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to like being part of that routine, feeling useful, talking to people. Lately I’ll look at it on the calendar and just feel this… heaviness, like, “Do I really have to go?”\n\nAnd the woodshop too. That used to be the place I could kind of settle myself. I’d tinker, fix something, make little adjustments. Now I’ll stand there looking at the tools and I just don’t have the pull toward it. It all feels like chores I keep putting off, even though these were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep’s been different. I can usually make myself go to bed at a reasonable time — I’m not up all night pacing or anything like that — but staying asleep is the problem.\n\nMost nights I wake up around two or three in the morning, and it’s like my mind is suddenly right back on that job site. I’ll be half-awake at first, and then all the details start coming back, and once that starts I’m just… up. I’ll lie there trying not to think about it, but that never really works.\n\nAnd then I get very aware of sounds in the house or outside. The furnace clicking on, a car going by, a board creaking — little normal noises, I know that, but at that hour they feel louder than they should. So instead of getting real rest, I’m lying there listening, waiting, trying to settle myself back down. Some nights I eventually doze off again, but it doesn’t feel like good sleep.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over a year now, I think. It started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress from everything changing at work, and that once things settled down I’d settle down too. But it didn’t really fade the way I expected. It’s been there in one form or another since then, not necessarily at the same intensity every single day, but enough that I can’t honestly say it ever went back to normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not a significant amount, anyway.\n\nI mean, my clothes don’t really fit differently, and nobody’s said anything like I look thinner. I haven’t been tracking my weight closely, so I can’t give you an exact number, but I don’t have the sense that I’ve lost a lot over the past year. If anything, it’s probably been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m constantly worrying about the future, no. I mean, I think about practical things like anyone does — work, money, family stuff, what needs to get done — but I don’t know that I sit around imagining every possible bad thing that could happen.\n\nIt’s more that when something specific is in front of me, I can get caught up in it a bit. But just in general, worrying about the future all the time… I haven’t really noticed that as the main issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Death or dying… I don’t know. Not in the sense of making plans or wanting to die, no. I wouldn’t say that.\n\nI mean, thoughts can come up in a more general way, like when something reminds me how things can go wrong suddenly, and then I’ll think about mortality or how fragile things are. But I don’t sit there thinking I’d be better off dead, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… unsettling thoughts, not something I’m seeking out or wanting.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound like everything is fine all the time, because I do get upset or overwhelmed sometimes, but hurting myself specifically… no. I haven’t been thinking about doing that, and I don’t have any plan or intention like that. If anything, when I get shaken up, I’m more trying to get through the moment than thinking about harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been moving unusually slowly, like people are commenting on it or I feel like I’m physically dragging in some obvious way. And I don’t think I’ve been sped up either, like talking a mile a minute or unable to sit still because my body is racing.\n\nMy thoughts can feel busy at times, but I wouldn’t describe it as “sped up” exactly. It’s more just… normal stress or trying to sort through things. But in terms of what you’re asking — moving slower or everything speeding up — no, I don’t think that’s been a big thing I’ve noticed lately.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually a big part of why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nI still know, intellectually, what I used to enjoy. Like sailing races — those used to be one of the things that kept me feeling like myself. I’d look forward to them, check the weather, think through the route, all of that. And my Saturday shift with the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a nice anchor in the week. Even being out in the woodshop, just tinkering with something, sanding or adjusting a piece until it was right… that was usually calming for me.\n\nBut lately it’s more like I look at those things and they feel like obligations. Chores. I keep putting them off, or I tell myself I’ll get back to them next weekend, and then I don’t. It’s not that I hate them now, exactly. I just don’t seem to care in the same way. The anticipation isn’t there. I don’t get that little lift from thinking about them. And that bothers me, because those were the things that used to keep me balanced.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your feelings of dread and guilt are interfering with your daily life most of the time, or is it more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, I think. Not every single second, but it’s there in the background a lot.\n\nIt’s like this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around during the day. I keep feeling like I should have seen one more risk coming, or caught one more thing before it became a problem, even though people keep telling me the controls were in place and that I did what I was supposed to do. I can hear that logically, but it doesn’t really settle it emotionally.\n\nSo it’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly falling apart all day. I can still get through things. But it’s there enough that it colors a lot of my normal routines. I’ll be doing something ordinary and then that feeling comes back — like I missed something, like I should have anticipated more — and it’s hard to just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say the dread and guilt are overwhelming at times or are they more of a persistent, manageable feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s more persistent than overwhelming, I’d say. I mean, there are moments where it feels heavier, where it kind of rises up and I get stuck on it, but most of the time it’s not like I’m completely incapacitated by it.\n\nIt’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt sitting in the background. Like I’m going through the day and some part of me is still reviewing everything, thinking, “You should have seen one more risk. You should have caught one more thing.” And the frustrating part is that people keep telling me the controls were in place, that it wasn’t because I ignored something or failed to do what I was supposed to do. I can repeat that back to myself, but it doesn’t really make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… manageable, technically, yes. I can function. But persistent. It follows me around more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, sure — stressful projects, family stuff, times where I was tired or discouraged or not enjoying things as much for a while. I don’t think I’d call those the same as this, though. Or at least I don’t remember them having this same kind of weight to them.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, not like this. But then I wonder if I’m minimizing earlier periods because I got through them and moved on. I’ve always been the sort of person who just keeps functioning and tells myself it’s just a rough patch. So I don’t know if I’ve ever really stopped and labeled it as “depressed.”\n\nBut as far as I can tell, this feels different enough that it’s what got my attention. It’s not just being in a slump for a week or two. It feels more persistent, more tied into everything. So… I guess my best answer is, not that I can clearly remember in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe tiny moments here and there, but nothing I actually look forward to the way I used to.\n\nThat’s honestly part of what finally made me agree to come in. I used to have things that kind of kept me balanced. Sailing races, for one. I’d plan around them, check conditions, fuss with gear, talk strategy — even if I was busy, I wanted to be there. And my Saturday shift at the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a quiet thing I did that connected me to something outside of work. I just don’t seem to care about them the way I did, and that worries me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I mean, there are moments where it feels heavier, where it kind of rises up and I get stuck on it, but most of the time it’s not like I’m completely incapacitated by it.\n\nIt’s more like this constant low-grade dread and guilt sitting in the background. Like I’m going through the day and some part of me is still reviewing everything, thinking, “You should have seen one more risk. You should have caught one more thing.” And the frustrating part is that people keep telling me the controls were in place, that it wasn’t because I ignored something or failed to do what I was supposed to do. I can repeat that back to myself, but it doesn’t really make the feeling go away.\n\nSo… manageable, technically, yes. I can function. But persistent. It follows me around more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling depressed like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches before, sure — stressful projects, family stuff, times where I was tired or discouraged or not enjoying things as much for a while. I don’t think I’d call those the same as this, though. Or at least I don’t remember them having this same kind of weight to them.\n\nPart of me wants to say no, not like this. But then I wonder if I’m minimizing earlier periods because I got through them and moved on. I’ve always been the sort of person who just keeps functioning and tells myself it’s just a rough patch. So I don’t know if I’ve ever really stopped and labeled it as “depressed.”\n\nBut as far as I can tell, this feels different enough that it’s what got my attention. It’s not just being in a slump for a week or two. It feels more persistent, more tied into everything. So… I guess my best answer is, not that I can clearly remember in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… maybe tiny moments here and there, but nothing I actually look forward to the way I used to.\n\nThat’s honestly part of what finally made me agree to come in. I used to have things that kind of kept me balanced. Sailing races, for one. I’d plan around them, check conditions, fuss with gear, talk strategy — even if I was busy, I wanted to be there. And my Saturday shift at the historical society used to feel meaningful, like a quiet thing I did that connected me to something outside of work. Even tinkering in the woodshop — that was always my way of decompressing.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… chores. Things I keep putting off. I’ll tell myself I should go, or I should get out to the shop for an hour, and then I just don’t. Or if I do, I’m kind of going through the motions and waiting until I can leave. It’s not that I hate those things now. I just don’t seem to care about them the way I did, and that worries me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this persistent low-grade dread and guilt?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / project_management_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on since the scaffold accident during the business readjustment, a little over a year now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It really started after the scaffold accident, during that whole business readjustment period. I keep trying to pin it down like there was a specific day it began, but it’s more like… after that happened, it just never fully lifted.

So, a little over a year now, I guess. Which sounds strange to say out loud, because for a while I kept treating it like it was just the aftermath of a bad incident, something that would settle down once the project stabilized or once enough time had passed. But it’s been more than a year, and that low-grade dread and guilt have kind of stayed there in the background the whole time.
